## 2. Retinal Artery Occlusion vs. Retinal Vein Occlusion
**Channel:** Dirty Medicine | **Views:** 6K | **Date:** 4 weeks ago | **Duration:** 6:34 | **ID:** EyolHpfyAnY
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=EyolHpfyAnY

### Transcript:
Medical students hate this question. Retinal artery occlusion versus retinal vein occlusion. The reason that this is so difficult is that both of these processes will present with acute painless moninocular vision loss. And yes, we can kind of nitpick here and maybe say that retinal vein occlusion tends to be a little bit more painful or maybe there's some temporal sparing in retinal artery occlusion. But the test writer knows this and they're not going to give you that. So in your exam, the way that you're going to get this right is if you can identify buzzwords and identify the picture when they show you the back of somebody's eye. So the way that I think you can simplify this is let's break it down and get back to basics. If you understand pathophysiology, this makes perfect sense. One of these has artery in the name. One of these has vein in the name. artery brings the blood to something and the vein takes it away. So if we block the artery, blood cannot get to the retina. If we block the vein, blood can't get out of the retina. And if we keep it that stupid and that simple, this all makes perfect sense. So in other words, a retinal artery occlusion where your artery is blocked, therefore blood can't get to something, that's a supply problem. a retinal vein occlusion, meaning that the blood cannot drain out of the eye, that's a drainage problem. So, let's pause for a second and I've got a really silly picture here at the bottom of the slide. If blood can't get in, what physical findings would you expect to see? And if blood can't get out, what physical findings would you expect to see? So taking that one step further in the artery occlusion you see a pale fundus with a cherry red spot. In the vein occlusion you see hemorrhage and edema. Now this should make sense to you when we look at hemorrhage and edema. If blood is pulling into the eye and can't get out through the retinal vein there will be hemorrhage and there will be edema specifically of the optic disc. And if we think about the pale fundus with a cherry red spot, because this is a supply problem, part of the eye is eskeemic. If you can't get blood to part of the eye, it will appear pale. And that should make sense to you. Now, as far as the cherry red spot goes, here's what you want to look for. You see the black arrow here pointing to the cherry red spot. And something that is important to understand here is that you see the reason that the cherry red spot is cherry red at the center but then pale around it is because the phobia will stay red. And the reason that the phobia stays red is that it gets its blood supply from the posterior siliary arteries. It does not get its blood supply from the central retinal artery. So basically anything that is supplied from the central retinal artery will become pale because it's es schemic. But anything that is supplied from the poster posterior siliary arteries will appear well perused because we don't have the blockage there. Again in a retinal artery occlusion the occlusion is in the retinal artery or the central retinal artery and this is why the phobia will be a nice dark red color but the surrounding tissue will be pale. Now let's contrast that to what you see in a retinal vein occlusion. So in a retinal vein occlusion, you're looking for optic disc edema. So the optic disc would swell and retinal hemorrhage. And you can see, I hope you can appreciate the very stark contrast between these two pictures. One is showing you hemorrhage and some swelling. The other shows you that cherry red spot with the pale fundus. And again, if you think about this as artery occlusion equals supply problem, it's eskeemic and vein equals drainage problem. it's buildup and swelling and hemorrhage. This really should make sense to you. Now, on your exam, they're going to show you one of these pictures like 90% of the time. They want you to be to pick out the cherry red spot. They want you to pick out the hemorrhage. And this is kind of basic. So, you really should commit this to memory. But if you're looking at causes here, the retinal artery occlusion typically is due to some type of thrombbo thrombboic event and the retinal vein occlusion is typically due to a compressive effect of nearby central retinal arteries having atherosclerosis. So interestingly in the retinal vein occlusion it is it is truly that a nearby artery becomes atherosclerotic and then that pushes up against the retinal vein which prevents the retinal vein from draining properly. Hence the hemorrhage hence the edema. But in the retinal artery occlusion the problem is inherent to whatever throboic event takes place that occludes the actual central retinal artery itself. And that's really important to understand. So, I've gone through this video and so far I would say that I've covered much of what you probably need to know for the purposes of your exam. Again, you want to be able to identify these pictures and know that the buzzwords pale fundus, cherry red spot equals artery occlusion, retinal hemorrhage, and optic disc edema equals vein occlusion. But if you need a way to memorize this and pair these together, I always think about artery is red and vein is blue. That's just how it is in my head, probably from years of studying this way. So the way that I memorize this is when I think about a pale fundus with a cherry red spot, pale to me pale is like a bucket. So I think about a bucket or a pale filled with cherries. And then I associate pale fundus, cherry red spot with red, you know, red cherries. That's the central artery. And everything else by process of elimination would be a retinal vein. So this was quick uh really to the point, but I want you to understand if you're blocking the artery, blood can't get in. That's a supply problem. So you're looking for eskeemic symptoms. If the vein is oluded, blood can't get out. That's a drainage problem. You're looking for hemorrhage and edema type symptoms. If you can keep that straight, then when you look at these images, you can kind of guess based on which blood vessel looks more engorged versus which one looks more like a cherry. So there you go. Good luck.

## 3. Creutzfeldt-Jakob Disease (CJD)

Let's learn everything that you need to know about Crutzfield Yakob disease. So CJD refers to a fatal rapidly neurodeenerative disease that is due to misfolded pron proteins. CJD is the most common pron disease in humans. It's considered technically a trans transmissible spongififor in sephylopathy. It can be subdivided into a couple different types. Sporadic type is the most common. It accounts for about 85% of all cases. In the sporadic type, there's no known cause. There's familial type, which is somewhere around 14 to maybe 15% of all cases. This is due to a mutation in the PR RNP gene that causes glutamic acid to get replaced by lysine and then that leads to formation of misfolded prons which we'll talk about in pathophysiology. And then lastly, there is the acquired subtype. Um, not really relevant for the purposes of exams, but just kind of an interesting aside. Acquired is when there's things like a contaminated uh piece of equipment in certain surgical procedures. So, this has historically been associated with things like corial transplants, for example. So, the big the big thing to take away from CJD is the pathofysiology here. So normally there are prons um they get encoded by the gene I mentioned them on the last slide it's the prnp gene and they exist in alpha helical configurations these are found on the membranes or I guess the surfaces for the purposes of this illustration of cortical neurons and so what happens here is that when the protein when the pron gets misfolded and it goes from being mostly alpha helical to being beta pleated then that is where the problem arises. So there's this misfolded protein now that should be alpha helical but now it's in beta pleated sheets and when it does that it leads to a host of problems. So your normal prons which are alphah helical shown in orange get changed into more beta pleated shown in green. This makes the prons insoluble and when they're insoluble they are resistant to proteasis. So in other words they can't be broken down and so if they can't be broken down then what happens is they induce even more confirmational changes in the normal more alpha helical prons into these abnormally folded beta pleated sheets. And what happens is it's like a self-fulfilling prophecy here. you get more accumulation and more self-propagation of these misfolded proteins and over time that leads to plaque formation vacualization and ultimately cell death. So for this reason this is why this is sometimes referred to as a sponge form in sephylopathy because as the cell death occurs and as that vacualization occurs it leads to neuronal cell death. So bottom line here, the problem is that alphah helical proteins get turned into beta pleated proteins which causes even more confirmational change, self-propagation, plaque formation, vacuualization, and cell death. ## 4. Aphasia (Broca vs. Wernicke vs. Conduction vs. Global vs. Transcortical)
**Channel:** Dirty Medicine | **Views:** 15K | **Date:** 4 months ago | **Duration:** 19:45 | **ID:** HpOaVyhuMW4
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=HpOaVyhuMW4

### Transcript:
In this video, I will teach you everything that you need to know about aphasia to both pass your exams, so USML and complex, but also get a jump start on some basic terminology and key differences that you might find useful if you're doing something like neurology rotation or an internal medicine rotation. Aphasia is confusing because there are various different types of aphasia and many of them have overlapping features. So let's start with a brief overview. Big picture, aphasia refers to the inability to understand or form language. And the key here is that this is not related to a motor dysfunction. In other words, if there's some reason why motorically you cannot produce speech, that is not an aphasia. Aphasia is inherent to the ability to form, manipulate and understand the language in the brain, not the motor ability to project it outward. ## 12. TACO vs. TRALI (Pulmonary Transfusion Reactions)

For all psychiatric questions, you want to look at the timeline of the symptoms. So, I bolded that this is for the past 72 hours. In addition, what pushes you in the direction of this not being a psychiatric issue is that they tell you he has been spending time with a new group of friends lately and staying out all night. And what that implies is that the patient is doing something socially, behaviorally that is leading to these symptoms. And so if the test writer gives you a sentence like this, and again this is very common for them to write something along these lines, tell you that the patient either has some occupation, some hobby, right? They're doing something and that something is on the short term, right? last 72 hours and now they have what looks like psych psychiatric symptoms. So although this question is telling you the patient is psychotic, you need to recognize that that's not due to schizophrenia, schizophren brief psychotic or delusional disorder, but rather presumably because this patient is doing some type of psychosimulant drug. This is substanceinduced psychosis. Now, I did give you that the patient was tacocartic, had a slightly increased blood pressure, and that pupils were dilated. That suggests that you're dealing with something substance related. It does, of course, doesn't guarantee that. So, that in combination with patients out all night, presumably doing drugs, and these symptoms are only lasting for 72 hours. That should push you in the direction of this being substance induced. Now, on your exam, typically this will be either due to cocaine, methamphetamine, or PCP use. Sometimes the test writer will tell you that the patient is having tactile hallucinations, meaning that they are feeling things on their body that are not really there. Tactile hallucinations tend to strongly be associated with stimulant use. But I didn't put that in this question because I didn't want to make it more obvious than I think it already is. But let's look at the timeline. So let's say for a second you didn't know that this was substance induced psychotic disorder. Is it possible to rule out other potential answer choices? And when it comes to answering questions in the topic of psychiatry, you want to pay attention to the timeline. So this couldn't possibly be answer choices B, C, or E. E would be that the patient is only having a fixed delusion and nothing else. We can rule that out because we can see clearly here that the patient isn't just delusional, but he's also presumably hallucinating. So E is eliminated. Schizophrenia for the timeline has to be six or more months of symptoms. And we can see here that this is only 72 hours. Likewise, schizophren disorder is 1 to 6 months. 1 to 6 months. So, we can rule that out. So, we're pretty much choosing between answer choice A, brief psychotic disorder, which is up to 1 month of a primary psychiatric issue versus choice D, substance induced psychotic disorder. And because again we know that the patient is likely using drugs, there's some social risk factor here that points us in the direction of answer choice D. So big takeaway again on your exam, be ready to answer questions that look like it's a psychiatry question, but it's going to make you pick out that it's actually substance induced or neurologic or medical and not actually one of your DSM diagnoses in psychiatry. Good luck.

## 30. Antibody Structure & Function
**Channel:** Dirty Medicine | **Views:** 102K | **Date:** 1 year ago | **Duration:** 11:06 | **ID:** rei0JHNN55E
**Link:** https://youtube.com/watch?v=rei0JHNN55E

in this video I will teach you everything that you need to know about the structure and to some extent the function of an antibody now you've probably seen an image that looks like this you look at this image and you know that this is an antibody but there are so many different parts of the antibody so many different regions each responsible for different functions that even if you know that this is an antibody that's honestly not enough for USM and comlex so the purpose of this video is to take a deep dive into the nitty-gritty details and simplify all of this pointing out high yield facts along the way so broadly speaking there are two different regions of the antibody the bottom region is the FC fragment and the top region is the Fab fragment the reason that these are called FC and Fab is that the C in FC stands for crystallizable don't even worry about the word crystallizable it's pretty much Irrelevant for the purposes of USMLE and complex the reason that it's faab is AB stands for antigen binding so right off the bat one really high yield topic to know is that the top region the Fab fragment that is the region of the antibody that contains the antigen binding site now in the center of the antibody connecting the top and the bottom region is this kinked area known as the hinge region now just as the name implies and just as the hinge of a door does the same function this allows the anab body to assume various formations and shapes which in short allows it to carry out its immune function better now if we look at the antibody at the top and the bottom we can highlight heavy chains shown here in purple there are heavy chains in the Fab fragment and heavy chains in the f C fragment there are also light chains which I put in this sort of Plum color here
